Source
Neutral status. The abc conjecture is not proved here. The project neither assumes that inter-universal Teichmüller theory is correct nor that it is wrong; a countermodel to a simplified abstraction is not a counterexample to the papers.

Reference

Every Lean file of the commit: Challenge.lean, Challenge/Defs.lean, Solution.lean and the files under IUT/, grouped by topic. Statements are copied from the source without their proofs; private declarations are omitted. This listing is read from the source text, not from the compiled library; the inventory of the compiled library is data/lean_declarations.json.

Trusted definitions

Challenge/Defs.lean: rad and the abc conjecture in three formulations, on Mathlib only. A reader must check by hand that these definitions say what the abc conjecture says.

Challenge.Defs

Challenge/Defs.lean

Trusted definitions

Module documentation

This file is the reviewed, protected vocabulary of the project. It depends on Mathlib only. Every definition here is meant to be read by a human and compared with its source. The file data/challenge_fingerprints.json records a fingerprint of each declaration, and the trust checks fail when a fingerprint changes without the record being updated on purpose.

Sources: the classical statement of the abc conjecture (Masser, Oesterlé), and Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory IV, Theorem A and Corollary 2.3, for the role the statement plays in the four papers.

Public declarations

Challenge.raddef

def rad (n : ℕ) : ℕ := ∏ p ∈ n.primeFactors, p

The radical of a natural number: the product of its distinct prime divisors. rad 0 = 1 and rad 1 = 1, because Nat.primeFactors is empty in both cases.

Challenge.ABCdef

def ABC : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ K : ℝ, 0 < K ∧ ∀ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a → 0 < b → 0 < c →
    Nat.Coprime a b → a + b = c → (c : ℝ) ≤ K * (rad (a * b * c) : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε)

The abc conjecture, constant form. For every real ε > 0 there is a real constant K > 0, depending on ε only, such that every triple of positive integers a, b, c with a and b coprime and a + b = c satisfies c ≤ K * rad (a * b * c) ^ (1 + ε). Because a + b = c, coprimality of a and b is the same as pairwise coprimality of the three numbers; see Solution.coprime_iff_pairwise. The exponent is a real power.

Challenge.ABCFinitedef

def ABCFinite : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → {t : ℕ × ℕ × ℕ | 0 < t.1 ∧ 0 < t.2.1 ∧ 0 < t.2.2 ∧
    Nat.Coprime t.1 t.2.1 ∧ t.1 + t.2.1 = t.2.2 ∧
    ((rad (t.1 * t.2.1 * t.2.2) : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε) < t.2.2)}.Finite

The abc conjecture, finiteness form: for every ε > 0 only finitely many coprime triples a + b = c have c > rad (a * b * c) ^ (1 + ε). This is the primary form in the Formal Conjectures project (FormalConjectures/Wikipedia/ABC.lean, theorem ABC.abc), with the pairwise coprimality written there replaced by the equivalent coprimality of a and b.

Challenge.ABCLogdef

def ABCLog : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a → 0 < b → 0 < c →
    Nat.Coprime a b → a + b = c →
      Real.log c ≤ (1 + ε) * Real.log (rad (a * b * c)) + C

The abc conjecture, logarithmic form: log c ≤ (1 + ε) * log (rad (a b c)) + C. This is the shape in which the statement appears as a height inequality (compare the inequality ht ≲ (1 + ε)(log-diff + log-cond) of IUT IV, Theorem A, for the projective line minus three points and rational points).

Challenge statements

Challenge.lean: the seven statements that lake comparator checks, each stated with sorry on purpose. It is not a default target and the axiom gate does not check it. Each statement needs a human comparison with its source.

Challenge

Challenge.lean · imports Challenge.Defs

Challenge: the headline statements

Module documentation

The challenge file of lake comparator (see comparator.json). It states the headline theorems of the project with sorry on purpose; Solution.lean states the same theorems, byte for byte and under the same names, and proves them from the library. The comparator checks that each solution theorem has the statement given here, with the definitions of Challenge.Defs underneath, uses no axiom outside propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound, and is accepted by the kernel.

This file imports only Challenge.Defs, so every statement below is written with Mathlib terms and the reviewed definitions Challenge.rad, Challenge.ABC, Challenge.ABCFinite and Challenge.ABCLog. Definitions of the library that a statement needs are written out in place. No statement here asserts the abc conjecture or its negation; the first two are equivalences between formulations.

This file is not part of any default build target, and nothing imports it. The sorry placeholders are allowed here and nowhere else outside IUT/Experimental/.

No PDF page was read for this file: the statements are taken from the library modules named below, whose docstrings record the pages read. Sources of the statements:

  • the equivalences and the radical: IUT/Applications/ABC.lean;
  • the final inequality of IUT IV, Theorem 1.10, as real arithmetic: IUT/PaperIV/Theorem1_10/Arithmetic.lean, theorem final_inequality, with the definition constant (the constant C_Θ of the statement) written out;
  • the two consequences of Scholze and Stix, Section 1.3, displays (1.4) to (1.6): IUT/Audit/ScholzeStix/Section1_3.lean, with the definitions thetaWithSquares and thetaWithoutSquares written out.
Public declarations

Solution.abc_iff_abcFinitetheorem

theorem abc_iff_abcFinite : ABC ↔ ABCFinite

The constant form and the finiteness form of the abc conjecture are equivalent.

theorem abc_iff_abcLog : ABC ↔ ABCLog

The constant form and the logarithmic form of the abc conjecture are equivalent.

Solution.rad_eq_radicaltheorem

theorem rad_eq_radical (n : ℕ) : rad n = UniqueFactorizationMonoid.radical n

The radical of Challenge.Defs is Mathlib's radical on ℕ.

Solution.coprime_iff_pairwisetheorem

theorem coprime_iff_pairwise {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  a.Coprime b ↔ ({a, b, c} : Set ℕ).Pairwise Nat.Coprime

For positive a, b with a + b = c, coprimality of a and b is pairwise coprimality of a, b, c.

Solution.theorem1_10_final_arithmetictheorem

theorem theorem1_10_final_arithmetic {l d D E Q q : ℝ} (hl : 7 ≤ l) (hd : 1 ≤ d)
  (hD : 0 ≤ D) (hE : 0 ≤ E) (hq : 0 < q)
  (hC : -1 ≤ (l + 1) / (4 * q) * ((1 + 12 * d / l) * D + 10 * E
    - 1 / 6 * (1 - 12 / l ^ 2) * Q) - 1) :
  1 / 6 * Q ≤ (1 + 20 * d / l) * D + 20 * E

IUT IV, Theorem 1.10, final portion, as real arithmetic: if the constant C_Θ of the statement is at least −1, then (1/6)·log(𝔮) ≤ (1 + 20·d_mod/l)·(log 𝔡 + log 𝔣) + 20·(e*_mod·l + η_prm).

Solution.scholzeStix_with_squarestheorem

theorem scholzeStix_with_squares {lh : ℕ} (hlh : 1 ≤ lh) {l : ℝ} (hl : l = 2 * lh + 1)
  {d qE : ℝ} (h14 : -(qE / (2 * l)) ≤
    (1 / (lh : ℝ)) * ∑ j ∈ Finset.Icc 1 lh, ((j : ℝ) * d - (j : ℝ) ^ 2 * (qE / (2 * l)))) :
  1 / 6 * qE * (1 - 12 / (l * (l + 1))) ≤ d

Scholze and Stix, Section 1.3: substituting the average (1.5), with the factor j², into (1.4) gives (1/6)·qE·(1 − 12/(ℓ(ℓ+1))) ≤ d.

Solution.scholzeStix_without_squarestheorem

theorem scholzeStix_without_squares {lh : ℕ} (hlh : 1 ≤ lh) (l d qE : ℝ) :
  -(qE / (2 * l)) ≤ (1 / (lh : ℝ)) * ∑ j ∈ Finset.Icc 1 lh, ((j : ℝ) * d - qE / (2 * l))
    ↔ 0 ≤ d

Scholze and Stix, Section 1.3: substituting the average (1.6), without the factor j², into (1.4) gives an inequality equivalent to 0 ≤ d.

  (hD : 0 ≤ D) (hE : 0 ≤ E) (hq : 0 < q)

Foundations

Grothendieck universes on ZFSet and the existence axiom of IUT IV, Section 3. The two existence results take that axiom as an explicit hypothesis.

IUT.Foundations.GrothendieckUniverse

IUT/Foundations/GrothendieckUniverse.lean

Grothendieck universes and the existence axiom of IUT IV, Section 3

Module documentation

Source: Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory IV, Section 3 (introductory discussion before Definition 3.1), PDF pages 67 and 68 (both rendered and read).

On page 67 the paper recalls that a (Grothendieck) universe V is a set satisfying five axioms (i) to (v), citing [McLn], page 194, and introduces the existence axiom (†G): every set is an element of some universe. A model of ZFC satisfying (†G) is called a ZFCG-model. The paper then derives two consequences of (†G): any set-indexed family of universes is contained, elementwise, in a single larger universe, and in particular there are infinite ascending chains V₀ ∈ V₁ ∈ V₂ ∈ ⋯ ∈ V. On page 68 it notes that, by the axiom of foundation, there are no infinite descending chains V₀ ∋ V₁ ∋ V₂ ∋ ⋯.

This file formalizes the definition and these three remarks over Mathlib's model ZFSet of ZFC set theory. It is not a formalization of the language of species (Definition 3.1 and after), of set-theoretic formulas, or of the "absoluteness" proviso on page 68.

What is and is not proved
  • IsGrothendieckUniverse transcribes axioms (i) to (v) of page 67.
  • The existence axiom (†G) is the proposition ExistenceAxiom. It is not proved: it is not a theorem of ZFC, and nothing here shows that ZFSet.{u} satisfies it. The paper assumes it (page 67) and explicitly leaves aside whether ZFCG-models exist (page 68). The results that use it, exists_universe_forall_mem and exists_ascending_chain, take it as an explicit hypothesis.
  • not_exists_descending_mem_chain is unconditional: it follows from the well-foundedness of ∈ on ZFSet (Mathlib's ZFSet.mem_wf), the formal counterpart of the axiom of foundation. It is proved for arbitrary sets, which covers chains of universes.
  • No Grothendieck universe is exhibited here, so the definition is not shown to be inhabited.
Status of ExistenceAxiom in the model ZFSet.{u}, and the risk of vacuity

ExistenceAxiom.{u} is a statement about the particular model ZFSet.{u}, not about the paper's axiom (†G) in the paper's set theory. Neither it nor its negation is proved here, and we do not expect either to be provable in Lean. The reason is an argument about models of Lean's type theory, which is not formalized: in the standard set-theoretic semantics the universe Type u is interpreted by V_κ for an inaccessible cardinal κ, and ZFSet.{u} then corresponds to V_κ. A Grothendieck universe that is an element of V_κ and contains ω is V_λ for an inaccessible λ < κ.

  • If κ is the least inaccessible cardinal, ZFSet.{u} contains no Grothendieck universe at all, so ExistenceAxiom.{u} is false. In such a model the two conditional theorems exists_universe_forall_mem and exists_ascending_chain are vacuous: their hypothesis fails.
  • If κ is an inaccessible limit of inaccessible cardinals, every element of V_κ lies in some V_λ with λ < κ inaccessible, so ExistenceAxiom.{u} holds. The existence of such a model needs a stronger consistency assumption than the consistency of Lean itself.

So, assuming the consistency of ZFC with enough inaccessible cardinals of the second kind, ExistenceAxiom.{u} is independent of Lean, and the two conditional theorems carry content only in models where it holds. A refutation of ExistenceAxiom was attempted along the lines "an ascending chain gives W containing all V n, and iterating would require unboundedly many universes"; it does not work, because no single set of ZFSet.{u} has rank cofinal in the model, and unboundedly many universes below κ is exactly what the second kind of model provides.

Interpretation choices, recorded
  • "The set of natural numbers ℕ" in axiom (ii) is Mathlib's von Neumann ZFSet.omega.
  • In axiom (v), a surjection f : x → y is a set f that is a function from x to y in the sense of Mathlib's ZFSet.IsFunc x y f (a subset of x × y that is single-valued and total on x) and whose image is all of y.
  • In exists_universe_forall_mem, the index set I of the paper is a type I that is u-small, so that the family i ↦ Vᵢ has a set of values (ZFSet.range). This is the formal counterpart of the paper's remark that the graph of the function I ∋ i ↦ Vᵢ is a set. The conclusion holds for any family of sets, not only for a family of universes; the paper's statement is the special case.
Public declarations

IUT.Foundations.IsSurjectiondef

def IsSurjection (x y f : ZFSet.{u}) : Prop :=
  IsFunc x y f ∧ ∀ b ∈ y, ∃ a ∈ x, pair a b ∈ f

A set f is a surjection from x onto y: it is a function from x to y (ZFSet.IsFunc), and every element of y is the value of f at some element of x.

IUT.Foundations.IsGrothendieckUniversestructure

structure IsGrothendieckUniverse (V : ZFSet.{u}) : Prop where

A (Grothendieck) universe, with the five axioms of IUT IV, page 67: (i) V is transitive; (ii) ℕ ∈ V; (iii) V is closed under power sets; (iv) V is closed under unions of its elements; (v) if x ∈ V, y ⊆ V, and f : x → y is a surjection, then y ∈ V.

IUT.Foundations.ExistenceAxiomdef

def ExistenceAxiom : Prop :=
  ∀ x : ZFSet.{u}, ∃ V, IsGrothendieckUniverse V ∧ x ∈ V

The existence axiom (†G) of the Grothendieck school (IUT IV, page 67), read in the model ZFSet.{u}: every set is an element of some universe that is itself an element of ZFSet.{u}. This is an assumption of the paper, not a theorem of ZFC. Neither it nor its negation is proved here; it is false in some models of Lean and true in others (see the module docstring), so theorems that assume it are vacuous in the former.

IUT.Foundations.exists_universe_forall_memtheorem

theorem exists_universe_forall_mem (hG : ExistenceAxiom.{u}) {I : Type*} [Small.{u} I]
  (V : I → ZFSet.{u}) : ∃ W, IsGrothendieckUniverse W ∧ ∀ i, V i ∈ W

IUT IV, page 67: under the existence axiom (†G) (which may fail in ZFSet.{u}; see the module docstring), any family of sets Vᵢ indexed by a set I is contained, elementwise, in a single universe V, that is, Vᵢ ∈ V for all i ∈ I. The paper states this for a family of universes; the proof does not use that the Vᵢ are universes. As in the paper, the proof applies (†G) to the set {Vᵢ} and then uses transitivity (axiom (i)).

IUT.Foundations.exists_ascending_chaintheorem

theorem exists_ascending_chain (hG : ExistenceAxiom.{u}) :
  ∃ V : ℕ → ZFSet.{u}, (∀ n, IsGrothendieckUniverse (V n)) ∧ (∀ n, V n ∈ V (n + 1)) ∧
    ∃ W, IsGrothendieckUniverse W ∧ ∀ n, V n ∈ W

IUT IV, page 67: under the existence axiom (†G) (which may fail in ZFSet.{u}; see the module docstring), there is an infinite ascending chain of universes V₀ ∈ V₁ ∈ V₂ ∈ ⋯ ∈ Vₙ ∈ ⋯ ∈ V, where V is itself a universe.

IUT.Foundations.not_exists_descending_mem_chaintheorem

theorem not_exists_descending_mem_chain :
  ¬ ∃ V : ℕ → ZFSet.{u}, ∀ n, V (n + 1) ∈ V n

IUT IV, page 68: by the axiom of foundation there is no infinite descending chain V₀ ∋ V₁ ∋ V₂ ∋ ⋯. Proved for arbitrary sets, hence in particular for universes, from the well-foundedness of ∈ on ZFSet.

The abc conjecture

Unconditional facts about the formulations of the abc conjecture.

IUT.Applications.ABC

IUT/Applications/ABC.lean · imports Challenge.Defs

The abc conjecture: basic facts and equivalent formulations

Module documentation

Everything here is unconditional. Nothing in this file says anything about whether the abc conjecture holds. The file proves that the three formulations in Challenge.Defs are equivalent, that the radical defined there is the one in Mathlib, and that the coprimality hypothesis can be written in either of the two usual ways.

Public declarations

IUT.ABC.rad_eq_radicaltheorem

theorem rad_eq_radical (n : ℕ) : rad n = UniqueFactorizationMonoid.radical n

The radical of Challenge.Defs is Mathlib's radical on ℕ.

theorem rad_pos (n : ℕ) : 0 < rad n

IUT.ABC.rad_dvdtheorem

theorem rad_dvd (n : ℕ) : rad n ∣ n

IUT.ABC.rad_letheorem

theorem rad_le {n : ℕ} (hn : 0 < n) : rad n ≤ n

IUT.ABC.rad_zerotheorem

theorem rad_zero : rad 0 = 1

IUT.ABC.rad_onetheorem

theorem rad_one : rad 1 = 1

IUT.ABC.rad_prime_powtheorem

theorem rad_prime_pow {p k : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hk : k ≠ 0) : rad (p ^ k) = p

IUT.ABC.rad_mul_of_coprimetheorem

theorem rad_mul_of_coprime {a b : ℕ} (h : a.Coprime b) : rad (a * b) = rad a * rad b

IUT.ABC.one_le_rad_casttheorem

theorem one_le_rad_cast (n : ℕ) : (1 : ℝ) ≤ rad n

IUT.ABC.one_le_rad_rpowtheorem

theorem one_le_rad_rpow (n : ℕ) {y : ℝ} (hy : 0 ≤ y) : (1 : ℝ) ≤ (rad n : ℝ) ^ y

IUT.ABC.coprime_iff_pairwisetheorem

theorem coprime_iff_pairwise {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  a.Coprime b ↔ ({a, b, c} : Set ℕ).Pairwise Nat.Coprime

For positive a, b with a + b = c, coprimality of a and b is the same as pairwise coprimality of a, b, c in the form used by the Formal Conjectures project. Positivity is needed: for a = b = c = 0 the set {a, b, c} is a singleton, so it is pairwise coprime, while 0 and 0 are not coprime.

IUT.ABC.abcLog_of_abctheorem

theorem abcLog_of_abc (h : ABC) : ABCLog

The constant form implies the logarithmic form, with C = log K.

IUT.ABC.abc_of_abcLogtheorem

theorem abc_of_abcLog (h : ABCLog) : ABC

The logarithmic form implies the constant form, with K = exp C.

IUT.ABC.abc_iff_abcLogtheorem

theorem abc_iff_abcLog : ABC ↔ ABCLog

IUT.ABC.abc_of_abcFinitetheorem

theorem abc_of_abcFinite (h : ABCFinite) : ABC

The finiteness form implies the constant form. The constant is one plus a bound for the values of c over the finitely many exceptional triples.

IUT.ABC.abcFinite_of_abctheorem

theorem abcFinite_of_abc (h : ABC) : ABCFinite

The constant form implies the finiteness form. One applies the constant form with ε / 2: an exceptional triple for ε then has rad ^ (ε / 2) < K, which bounds the radical, hence c, hence a and b.

IUT.ABC.abc_iff_abcFinitetheorem

theorem abc_iff_abcFinite : ABC ↔ ABCFinite

IUT.ABC.hypotheses_nonvacuoustheorem

theorem hypotheses_nonvacuous :
  ∃ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ Nat.Coprime a b ∧ a + b = c

The set of triples the conjecture quantifies over is not empty: 1 + 1 = 2. So no formulation above is true for the empty reason that nothing satisfies its hypotheses.

IUT.Applications.ABCFormalConjectures

IUT/Applications/ABCFormalConjectures.lean · imports IUT.Applications.ABC

Comparison with the abc statements of the Formal Conjectures project

Module documentation

Source: the file FormalConjectures/Wikipedia/ABC.lean of https://github.com/google-deepmind/formal-conjectures, at commit 9097e2f90e66d2f6244209cb1c07f7120d17ec53 (2026-11-24). The file is byte-identical to the version at commit 1646ca16afd6cc7a693d3bdc9f066c4d3cc01a89, the commit cited by the lana-agents/iut repository. The project is licensed under Apache-2.0 (Copyright 2025 The Formal Conjectures Authors).

That file cannot be imported here (it is a Lean module of a different project, on Lean v4.33.1, and its theorems are stated with sorry). This file restates, in the namespace IUT.ABC.FormalConjectures, their definitions ABC.radical and ABC.quality with the same bodies, and turns each of their three open theorems into a definition of type Prop whose body is the theorem statement with the arguments (ε : ℝ) (hε : 0 < ε) written as ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →; binders, set-builders and coercions are as in their file:

  • abc (their ABC.abc): finiteness of the exceptional triples, with pairwise coprimality of the set {a, b, c};
  • ltConstantMul (their ABC.abc.variants.lt_constant_mul): a real constant K, with no sign condition, and the strict inequality c < K * rad(abc)^(1 + ε);
  • qualityVariant (their ABC.abc.variants.quality): finiteness of the triples of quality q(a, b, c) = log c / log rad(abc) greater than 1 + ε.

Each is proved equivalent to Challenge.ABC. Nothing here says whether any of them holds.

Remarks on the formulations, all checked by the proofs below.

  • Coprimality: for positive a, b with a + b = c, pairwise coprimality of {a, b, c} is coprimality of a and b (IUT.ABC.coprime_iff_pairwise).
  • Constant form: Challenge.ABC asks for 0 < K and the non-strict ≤; theirs asks for no sign and the strict <. These are equivalent: the triple 1 + 1 = 2 forces K > 0 in their form, and K + 1 turns ≤ into < because rad(abc)^(1 + ε) ≥ 1.
  • Quality form: Lean's division gives x / 0 = 0, so the quality is 0 when rad(abc) = 1. For positive a, b with a + b = c this never happens: c ≥ 2, so rad(abc) ≥ 2 (rad(abc) = 1 with abc ≠ 0 forces abc = 1, hence a = b = c = 1, and 1 + 1 ≠ 1). When rad(abc) ≥ 2 the logarithm of the radical is positive, and q > 1 + ε is exactly rad(abc)^(1 + ε) < c. So, for each ε, the quality set is the same set as the set of the finiteness form (qualitySet_eq), and the quality form is exactly equivalent; no strict versus non-strict discrepancy arises, since both sets use strict inequalities.

No PDF page of the IUT papers is used in this file.

The radical of n denoted is the product of the distinct prime factors of n. (Their ABC.radical, same body. Named fcRadical here: a project constant radical would shadow Ideal.radical in any file of this namespace that opens Ideal.)

Quality q(a, b, c) of the triple (a, b, c) is defined as q(a,b,c) = log (c) / log (rad(abc)). (Their ABC.quality, same body.)

IUT.ABC.FormalConjectures.abcdef

def abc : Prop := ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →
  {(a, b, c) : ℕ × ℕ × ℕ | 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ ({a, b, c} : Set ℕ).Pairwise Nat.Coprime ∧
  a + b = c ∧ (fcRadical <| a * b * c : ℝ)^(1 + ε) < c}.Finite

For every positive real number ε, there exist only finitely many triples (a, b, c) of coprime positive integers, with a + b = c, such that c > rad(abc)^(1+ε). (Statement of their theorem ABC.abc.)

IUT.ABC.FormalConjectures.ltConstantMuldef

def ltConstantMul : Prop := ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ K,
  ∀ (a b c : ℕ), 0 < a → 0 < b → 0 < c → ({a, b, c} : Set ℕ).Pairwise Nat.Coprime →
  a + b = c → c < K * (fcRadical <| a * b * c : ℝ)^(1 + ε)

For every positive real number ε, there exists a constant K_ε such that for all triples (a, b, c) of coprime positive integers, with a + b = c we have c < K_ε rad(abc)^(1+ε). (Statement of their theorem ABC.abc.variants.lt_constant_mul.)

IUT.ABC.FormalConjectures.abcSet_eqtheorem

theorem abcSet_eq (ε : ℝ) :
  {(a, b, c) : ℕ × ℕ × ℕ | 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ ({a, b, c} : Set ℕ).Pairwise Nat.Coprime ∧
    a + b = c ∧ (fcRadical <| a * b * c : ℝ)^(1 + ε) < c} =
  {t : ℕ × ℕ × ℕ | 0 < t.1 ∧ 0 < t.2.1 ∧ 0 < t.2.2 ∧
    Nat.Coprime t.1 t.2.1 ∧ t.1 + t.2.1 = t.2.2 ∧
    ((rad (t.1 * t.2.1 * t.2.2) : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε) < t.2.2)}

The set of the finiteness form of Formal Conjectures is the set of Challenge.ABCFinite.

theorem qualitySet_eq (ε : ℝ) :

IUT.Applications.ABCSpecialization

IUT/Applications/ABCSpecialization.lean · imports IUT.Applications.ABC

Rational points of the line minus three points and the abc conjecture

Module documentation

Source: Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory IV, Theorem A (page 3), the notation recalled before Corollary 2.2 (page 40), Corollary 2.2 (pages 41 to 43, read for the setting X = ℙ¹_ℚ, D = {0, 1, ∞}), and Corollary 2.3 with its proof and Remark 2.3.1 (pages 54 to 56). Second source: Mochizuki, Arithmetic Elliptic Curves in General Position ([GenEll]), §1 (Definitions 1.1, 1.2, Example 1.3, Proposition 1.4, Remark 1.4.1, Definition 1.5, Remark 1.5.1, Propositions 1.6 and 1.7; pages 3 to 10) and §2 (Theorem 2.1 and its proof; pages 11 to 13), with its list of corrections, Comments on "Arithmetic Elliptic Curves in General Position" (July 2016, pages 1 and 2), whose items (1), (2), (5) are repeated in IUT IV, Remark 2.3.1. Pages read for this file: IUT IV pages 3, 40 to 43, 54 to 56; [GenEll] pages 3 to 13; the corrections, pages 1 and 2. Pages checked against rendered images: all of these.

What this file proves. It proves that a statement about rational points of the projective line minus the three points 0, 1, ∞ (VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational) is equivalent to the abc conjecture Challenge.ABC (vojta_iff_ABC). It does not prove that statement. In the papers that statement is a special case of IUT IV, Corollary 2.3 (equivalently Theorem A), whose proof depends on IUT I to III and is not formalized here. Nothing here bears on whether that proof is correct.

The statement of the papers and what is dropped. Corollary 2.3 (page 54) concerns a smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve X over a number field, a reduced divisor D with U_X = X \ D hyperbolic, a positive integer d and ε > 0, and asserts the inequality of bounded discrepancy classes ht_{ω_X(D)} ≲ (1 + ε)(log-diff_X + log-cond_D) of functions on U_X(ℚ̄)^{≤d}, the points defined over number fields of degree at most d. Here α ≲ β means that α - β is bounded above by a constant (Remark 2.3.1 (ii), page 55). The proof (page 55) reduces, through [GenEll] Theorem 2.1, to X = ℙ¹_ℚ, D = {0, 1, ∞}, and points in a compactly bounded subset K_V of U_X(ℚ̄). The proposition of this file keeps one curve (X = ℙ¹_ℚ, D = {0, 1, ∞}) and one field (d = 1, points over ℚ), and drops: number fields of bounded degree d > 1, the compactly bounded subsets K_V of the proof, and general hyperbolic curves over number fields.

Normalizations, checked against [GenEll]. Page 40 of IUT IV says: ht_{ω_X(D)} is the normalized height on U_X(ℚ̄) attached to ω_X(D), well defined up to a bounded discrepancy ([GenEll] Proposition 1.4 (iii)); log-diff_X is the normalized log-different ([GenEll] Definition 1.5 (iii)); log-cond_D is the normalized log-conductor ([GenEll] Definition 1.5 (iv)). In [GenEll] these are defined on a normal, proper, flat model X over ℤ, and at a point x ∈ X(F) are computed with the normalized degree (1 / [F : ℚ]) · deg_F of arithmetic divisors on Spec 𝒪_F, which gives a finite prime v the weight log q_v (page 4). For a rational point (F = ℚ) the normalization is division by 1.

  • Height (page 4, page 6, page 8). ht_𝓜(x) is the normalized degree of the pullback of an arithmetic line bundle 𝓜 (a line bundle on the model with a hermitian metric) along Spec 𝒪_F → X. By Proposition 1.4 (iii) its bounded discrepancy class depends only on the isomorphism class of the line bundle on the generic fiber, and by Remark 1.4.1 only on X_ℚ. For X = ℙ¹_ℚ and D = {0, 1, ∞}, ω_X(D) has degree -2 + 3 = 1, so it is 𝒪(1). What [GenEll] does not contain, and this file does not formalize, is the computation that ht_{𝒪(1)} at x = a / c in lowest terms equals log max(|a|, |c|) up to a bounded function (for 𝒪_ℙ(1) on ℙ¹_ℤ with the Fubini-Study metric used on page 7 the value is (1/2) log (a² + c²), within (1/2) log 2 of log max(|a|, |c|)); that step is a standard computation recorded here as an unformalized link. This file uses Mathlib's Height.logHeight₁ on ℚ, which Mathlib proves equal to log max(|num x|, den x) (Rat.logHeight₁_eq_log_max). Since ≲ absorbs bounded functions, any function at bounded distance from it gives an equivalent statement (vojta_iff_rationalPointBoundFor).
  • Log-different (page 8, Definition 1.5 (iii)). It is the normalized degree of the arithmetic divisor of the different ideal of the minimal field of definition of x. For a rational point that field is ℚ, whose different is the unit ideal, so the log-different is exactly 0.
  • Log-conductor (page 8, Definition 1.5 (iv), with correction (12) of the comments: "conductor of x with respect to D"). Pull D back along Spec 𝒪_F → X to an effective divisor D_x; the conductor is (D_x)_red, an arithmetic divisor supported at the finite primes; the log-conductor is its normalized degree. So the archimedean place contributes nothing, each prime counts once whatever its multiplicity, and no prime (in particular not 2) is treated differently. For the model ℙ¹_ℤ with D the closure of the three sections 0, 1, ∞, a rational point x = n / d in lowest terms is the section (n : d), and D_x is cut out by n, n - d and d. Hence the log-conductor is the sum of log p over the primes dividing n, n - d (the numerator of x - 1) or d, which is log (ratConductor x) exactly. This pullback computation is made here, not quoted from [GenEll]. Another model changes the value, but by Remark 1.5.1 (pages 8 and 9) only at finitely many primes, hence by a bounded function.

The relation α ≲ β means α - β ≤ C for a constant C: this is [GenEll] Definition 1.2 (ii) (page 5) after correction (2) of the comments, which reverses the printed direction, repeated as IUT IV Remark 2.3.1 (ii) (page 55). So the inequality ht ≤ (1 + ε)(log-diff + log-cond) + C on rational points is the d = 1 case of the inequality of Corollary 2.3, up to the unformalized identification of the height named above.

[GenEll] Theorem 2.1 and what remains between this file and the papers. Theorem 2.1 (page 11) fixes a finite set Σ of prime numbers and asserts that two statements are equivalent. (i) For every smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve X over a number field, reduced divisor D with U_X = X \ D hyperbolic (deg ω_X(D) > 0), positive integer d and ε > 0, ht_{ω_X(D)} ≲ (1 + ε)(log-diff_X + log-cond_D) on U_X(ℚ̄)^{≤d}. (ii) For P = ℙ¹_ℚ, C = {0, 1, ∞}, every positive integer d, every ε > 0 and every compactly bounded subset K_V ⊆ U_P(ℚ̄) (Example 1.3 (ii), pages 5 and 6, with correction (8): its bounding domains lie in U_P) whose support contains Σ, ht_{ω_P(C)} ≲ (1 + ε)(log-diff_P + log-cond_C) on K_V ∩ U_P(ℚ̄)^{≤d}. The direction (i) ⟹ (ii) is immediate. The proof of (ii) ⟹ (i) (pages 11 to 13) passes to a finite étale Galois covering Y of U_X, which raises the degree bound to d' = d · deg(Y/X), reduces to D = ∅, uses compactness at the places of a finite set V and a noncritical Belyi map X → P (from an earlier paper of Mochizuki, cited as [Mzk1] Theorem 2.5), and Propositions 1.4, 1.6 and 1.7; correction (5) replaces the last ≈ of the final display by ht_E ≲ 2 · (deg E / deg ω_X) · ht_{ω_X} and adjusts the choice of ε'.

In IUT IV the chain is as follows. Corollary 2.2 (pages 41 to 43) constructs initial Θ-data to which Theorem 1.10 is applied (Theorem 1.10 itself and the proof of Corollary 2.2 beyond its first paragraph, page 43, are not among the pages read for this file). For X = ℙ¹_ℚ, D = {0, 1, ∞} and a compactly bounded K_V whose support contains the prime 2 and which satisfies a condition (*_{j-inv}) at 2, it identifies (1/6) log 𝔮^∀ with ht_{ω_X(D)} up to bounded discrepancy on K_V (part (i)); bounds (1/6) log 𝔮^∀ by (1 + ε_E)(log-diff_X + log-cond_D) + C_K for the points of K_V of degree at most d outside a finite set (part (ii), condition (C2); this summary omits the conditions of part (ii) on the field F and on the prime l, condition (C1)); and makes ε_E ≤ ε outside a finite set (part (iii)). The proof of Corollary 2.3 (pages 54 and 55) says that Corollary 2.3 coincides with [GenEll] Theorem 2.1 (i), so by the equivalence it suffices to prove (ii) with Σ = {2}; it adds the condition (*_{j-inv}), which the paper says is vacuous for fixed d, and derives (ii) from Corollary 2.2 (i), (ii), (iii). The proposition of this file is an instance of (i), not of (ii): it has no K_V. So reaching it from IUT IV uses the direction (ii) ⟹ (i) of [GenEll] Theorem 2.1. On our reading of page 12 (not a sentence of the paper), that proof uses (ii) at the degree d' > 1 of the covering even for d = 1 and X = ℙ¹, so a version of Corollary 2.2 for rational points alone would not suffice through this route. None of Theorem 1.10, Corollary 2.2, Theorem 2.1 of [GenEll], the Belyi map theorem, or the height computation above is formalized here.

Main results.

  • logHeight₁_of_triple, ratConductor_of_triple: for positive coprime a, b with a + b = c, the point x = a / c has height log c and conductor rad (a b c).
  • exists_triple_of_ne: every rational x ∉ {0, 1} gives such a triple, after clearing denominators and choosing signs, with height at most log c and conductor rad (a b c).
  • vojta_iff_ABC: the proposition is equivalent to Challenge.ABC.
  • ratConductor_one_sub, ratConductor_inv: the conductor is invariant under the maps x ↦ 1 - x and x ↦ 1 / x, which generate the six permutations of {0, 1, ∞}; abs_logHeight₁_one_sub_sub_le: the height changes by at most log 2 under x ↦ 1 - x (and not at all under x ↦ 1 / x, Mathlib's Height.logHeight₁_inv).
  • vojta_iff_unitInterval: the proposition is equivalent to its restriction to 0 < x < 1.
  • exists_rat_ne_zero_ne_one and the values at 1/2 and 8/9: the statement quantifies over a nonempty set and the definitions give the expected numbers.
Public declarations

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductordef

def ratConductor (x : ℚ) : ℕ :=
  ∏ p ∈ x.num.natAbs.primeFactors ∪ (x - 1).num.natAbs.primeFactors ∪ x.den.primeFactors, p

The conductor of a rational number x relative to the divisor {0, 1, ∞} of ℙ¹: the product of the primes p that divide the numerator of x (where x reduces to 0), the numerator of x - 1 (where x reduces to 1), or the denominator of x (where x reduces to ∞). Its logarithm is the log-conductor of the rational point x in the sense of [GenEll] Definition 1.5 (iv) (page 8) for the model ℙ¹_ℤ with the sections 0, 1, ∞; another model changes it by a bounded function (module docstring).

IUT.ABCSpecialization.VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRationaldef

def VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ x : ℚ, x ≠ 0 → x ≠ 1 →
    Height.logHeight₁ x ≤ (1 + ε) * Real.log (ratConductor x) + C

A special case of the conclusion of IUT IV, Corollary 2.3, not the corollary itself. For every ε > 0 there is a constant C such that every rational number x ≠ 0, 1 satisfies ht(x) ≤ (1 + ε) * log (ratConductor x) + C, where ht is Mathlib's logarithmic height on ℚ. This is the inequality ht_{ω_X(D)} ≲ (1 + ε)(log-diff_X + log-cond_D) of Corollary 2.3 (page 54) specialized to one curve, X = ℙ¹_ℚ with D = {0, 1, ∞}, and to its rational points (d = 1), where the log-different vanishes. It drops the points over number fields of degree d > 1, the compactly bounded subsets of the proof, and all other hyperbolic curves over number fields. It is an open statement: this file proves only that it is equivalent to the abc conjecture (vojta_iff_ABC).

IUT.ABCSpecialization.RationalPointBoundFordef

def RationalPointBoundFor (ht : ℚ → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ x : ℚ, x ≠ 0 → x ≠ 1 →
    ht x ≤ (1 + ε) * Real.log (ratConductor x) + C

The same inequality with an arbitrary function ht on ℚ in place of the height. Used only to show that changing the height by a bounded function does not change the statement.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.rationalPointBoundFor_iff_of_boundedtheorem

theorem rationalPointBoundFor_iff_of_bounded {ht ht' : ℚ → ℝ} (B : ℝ)
  (hB : ∀ x : ℚ, x ≠ 0 → x ≠ 1 → |ht x - ht' x| ≤ B) :
  RationalPointBoundFor ht ↔ RationalPointBoundFor ht'

If ht and ht' differ by at most B on ℚ \ {0, 1}, the two inequalities are equivalent: the constant C absorbs B.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.vojta_iff_rationalPointBoundFortheorem

theorem vojta_iff_rationalPointBoundFor {ht : ℚ → ℝ} (B : ℝ)
  (hB : ∀ x : ℚ, x ≠ 0 → x ≠ 1 → |ht x - Height.logHeight₁ x| ≤ B) :
  VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational ↔ RationalPointBoundFor ht

The proposition of this file is the bound for Mathlib's height, and any height at bounded distance from it on ℚ \ {0, 1} gives an equivalent statement.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.num_sub_one_eq_num_sub_dentheorem

theorem num_sub_one_eq_num_sub_den (x : ℚ) : (x - 1).num = x.num - x.den

The numerator of x - 1 is the numerator of x minus the denominator of x: the fraction (num x - den x) / den x is already in lowest terms.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.rad_mul_mul_eq_prod_uniontheorem

theorem rad_mul_mul_eq_prod_union {a b c : ℕ} (ha : a ≠ 0) (hb : b ≠ 0) (hc : c ≠ 0) :
  rad (a * b * c) = ∏ p ∈ a.primeFactors ∪ b.primeFactors ∪ c.primeFactors, p

The radical of a product of three nonzero numbers is the product over the union of their sets of prime factors.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.num_den_of_tripletheorem

theorem num_den_of_triple {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (hab : a.Coprime b)
  (h : a + b = c) : ((a : ℚ) / c).num = a ∧ ((a : ℚ) / c).den = c

For positive coprime a, b with a + b = c, the fraction a / c is in lowest terms: its numerator is a and its denominator is c.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.logHeight₁_of_tripletheorem

theorem logHeight₁_of_triple {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (hab : a.Coprime b)
  (h : a + b = c) : Height.logHeight₁ ((a : ℚ) / c) = Real.log c

Height of the point of a triple. For positive coprime a, b with a + b = c, the height of a / c is log c.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductor_of_tripletheorem

theorem ratConductor_of_triple {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (hab : a.Coprime b)
  (h : a + b = c) : ratConductor ((a : ℚ) / c) = rad (a * b * c)

Conductor of the point of a triple. For positive coprime a, b with a + b = c, the conductor of x = a / c is rad (a b c): the numerator of x is a, the numerator of x - 1 = -b / c is -b, and the denominator of x is c.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.exists_triple_of_netheorem

theorem exists_triple_of_ne (x : ℚ) (h0 : x ≠ 0) (h1 : x ≠ 1) :
  ∃ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ a.Coprime b ∧ a + b = c ∧
    Height.logHeight₁ x ≤ Real.log c ∧ ratConductor x = rad (a * b * c)

Every rational point of the line minus three points gives a triple. Write n, d for the numerator and denominator of x ∉ {0, 1}, so that n - d is the numerator of x - 1. The three numbers |n|, |n - d|, d are positive, and one of them is the sum of the other two: |n| + d = |n - d| if x < 0, |n| + |n - d| = d if 0 < x < 1, and |n - d| + d = |n| if 1 < x. This gives positive coprime a, b with a + b = c, the height of x is at most log c (with equality unless x < 0), and the conductor of x is rad (a b c).

IUT.ABCSpecialization.abcLog_of_vojtatheorem

theorem abcLog_of_vojta (h : VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational) : ABCLog

The bound on rational points implies the logarithmic form of abc, applied to x = a / c.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.vojta_of_abcLogtheorem

theorem vojta_of_abcLog (h : ABCLog) : VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational

The logarithmic form of abc implies the bound on rational points, through exists_triple_of_ne, with the same constant.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.vojta_iff_ABCtheorem

theorem vojta_iff_ABC : VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational ↔ ABC

**The bound on rational points of ℙ¹ \ {0, 1, ∞} is equivalent to the abc conjecture.** Neither side is proved here.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.num_one_sub_eq_den_sub_numtheorem

theorem num_one_sub_eq_den_sub_num (x : ℚ) : (1 - x).num = x.den - x.num

The numerator of 1 - x is the denominator of x minus the numerator of x.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.den_one_sub_eq_dentheorem

theorem den_one_sub_eq_den (x : ℚ) : (1 - x).den = x.den

The denominator of 1 - x is the denominator of x.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductor_one_subtheorem

theorem ratConductor_one_sub (x : ℚ) : ratConductor (1 - x) = ratConductor x

**The conductor is invariant under x ↦ 1 - x**, which exchanges 0 and 1 and fixes ∞: the numerators of 1 - x and (1 - x) - 1 = -x are, up to sign, those of x - 1 and x, and the denominator is unchanged.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductor_invtheorem

theorem ratConductor_inv (x : ℚ) : ratConductor x⁻¹ = ratConductor x

**The conductor is invariant under x ↦ 1 / x**, which exchanges 0 and ∞ and fixes 1: for x ≠ 0 the numerator and denominator of 1 / x are, up to sign, the denominator and numerator of x, and the numerator of 1 / x - 1 is, up to sign, that of x - 1. At x = 0 both sides are the conductor of 0, since 0⁻¹ = 0 in ℚ.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.abs_logHeight₁_one_sub_sub_letheorem

theorem abs_logHeight₁_one_sub_sub_le (x : ℚ) :

**The height changes by at most log 2 under x ↦ 1 - x.** With x = n / d in lowest terms, 1 - x = (d - n) / d in lowest terms, and each of max(|d - n|, d) and max(|n|, d) is at most twice the other. (Under x ↦ 1 / x the height is unchanged: Mathlib's Height.logHeight₁_inv.)

IUT.ABCSpecialization.VojtaLineMinusThreePointsUnitIntervaldef

def VojtaLineMinusThreePointsUnitInterval : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ x : ℚ, 0 < x → x < 1 →
    Height.logHeight₁ x ≤ (1 + ε) * Real.log (ratConductor x) + C

The inequality of VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational asked only for rational x with 0 < x < 1.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.vojta_iff_unitIntervaltheorem

theorem vojta_iff_unitInterval :
  VojtaLineMinusThreePointsRational ↔ VojtaLineMinusThreePointsUnitInterval

**It suffices to bound the points of (0, 1), with the same constant.** One direction is a restriction. For the other, exists_triple_of_ne sends x ∉ {0, 1} to a triple a + b = c; the point y = a / c lies in (0, 1), has height log c ≥ ht(x) and the same conductor rad (a b c) as x. (The point y is the image of x under one of the six maps generated by x ↦ 1 - x and x ↦ 1 / x, compare ratConductor_one_sub, ratConductor_inv and abs_logHeight₁_one_sub_sub_le, but the proof does not use those lemmas.)

IUT.ABCSpecialization.exists_rat_ne_zero_ne_onetheorem

theorem exists_rat_ne_zero_ne_one : ∃ x : ℚ, x ≠ 0 ∧ x ≠ 1

The set of rational points quantified over is not empty.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.logHeight₁_one_halftheorem

theorem logHeight₁_one_half : Height.logHeight₁ (1 / 2 : ℚ) = Real.log 2

The height of 1/2 is log 2 (triple 1 + 1 = 2).

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductor_one_halftheorem

theorem ratConductor_one_half : ratConductor (1 / 2 : ℚ) = 2

The conductor of 1/2 is 2: the numerator of 1/2 is 1, the numerator of -1/2 is -1, and the denominator is 2.

IUT.ABCSpecialization.logHeight₁_eight_ninthstheorem

theorem logHeight₁_eight_ninths : Height.logHeight₁ (8 / 9 : ℚ) = Real.log 9

The height of 8/9 is log 9 (triple 8 + 1 = 9).

IUT.ABCSpecialization.ratConductor_eight_ninthstheorem

theorem ratConductor_eight_ninths : ratConductor (8 / 9 : ℚ) = 6

The conductor of 8/9 is 6 = rad (8 * 1 * 9): the primes are 2 (numerator 8) and 3 (denominator 9); the numerator of 8/9 - 1 = -1/9 contributes none.

Variants of the abc statement

Signed and fixed exponent forms of the abc statement, each proved equivalent to Challenge.ABC, and elementary facts about abc triples. Nothing here says whether the abc conjecture holds.

IUT.ABC.Variants

IUT/ABC/Variants.lean · imports IUT.Applications.ABCFormalConjectures

Variants of the abc conjecture and elementary facts about abc triples

Module documentation

This file collects reformulations of the abc conjecture and elementary, unconditional facts about triples a + b = c that other formalizations commonly use as reference points. Nothing here says whether the abc conjecture holds. No page of the IUT papers is used.

**Reformulations, each proved equivalent to Challenge.ABC.**

  • ABCInt: the signed integer form, with nonzero pairwise coprime integers a + b + c = 0 and the bound max(|a|, |b|, |c|) ≤ K * rad(|abc|)^(1 + ε); ABCIntSum is the same with a + b = c and signs arbitrary. The shape (a constant K > 0 and the non-strict ≤) is the shape of Challenge.ABC, so the comparison is exact. Equivalences: abcInt_iff_ABC, abcIntSum_iff_ABC.
  • ABCWithExponent κ: the bound c ≤ K * rad(abc)^κ with one fixed exponent κ. The conjecture is ∀ ε > 0, ABCWithExponent (1 + ε) (abc_iff_forall_abcWithExponent, true by definition). For one fixed κ > 1 the statement ABCWithExponent κ is implied by the conjecture (abcWithExponent_of_ABC) and is strictly weaker as a statement: knowing it for one κ > 1 gives no bound with any smaller exponent, so it does not give the conjecture by any argument in this file. Results and claims with one fixed exponent larger than 1 (for example of the form 24 + ε) appear in the literature; each is a statement of this weaker shape, not the conjecture. For κ ≤ 1 the statement is false (not_abcWithExponent_of_le_one).

The quality form q(a, b, c) = log c / log rad(abc) and its finiteness statement are already in IUT.ABC.FormalConjectures (quality, qualityVariant, qualityVariant_iff_ABC); this file reuses that definition of the quality and does not restate the finiteness form.

Elementary facts.

  • Trivial bounds: 2 ≤ rad(abc) (two_le_rad_of_triple), rad(abc) ≤ abc (rad_le_mul_mul), abc < c^3 (mul_mul_lt_cube), and 6 ≤ rad(abc) once c ≥ 3 (six_le_rad_of_three_le); the triple 1 + 1 = 2 with rad = 2 shows that c ≥ 3 is needed (rad_one_one_two). The bound c < rad(abc)^2 is a conjecture and is not stated.
  • Lemma 2.2 of R. Laniewski and K. Müller, The quality plane of abc-triples and the decomposition into gains (page 2, read and checked against a rendered image). Their triples are coprime positive a + b = c with a < b, and their balance is λ = log(abc) / log c. The lemma says 2 < λ < 3 when a ≥ 2 and 2 - 1 / ((c - 1) log c) < λ < 2 when a = 1. All four inequalities are proved here, multiplied out by log c > 0: log_mul_mul_lt_three_mul_log, two_mul_log_lt_log_mul_mul, log_mul_mul_lt_two_mul_log_of_one, two_mul_log_sub_lt_log_mul_mul_of_one. The hypothesis a ≥ 2 of the lower bound λ > 2 is needed (1 + 2 = 3: abc = 6 < 9 = c^2), and so is a < b, which the lemma takes from the definition of a triple (2 + 2 = 4: abc = 16 = c^2, so λ = 2). Coprimality is not used by the lower bound once a < b.
  • Classical examples: 1 + 8 = 9 with rad = 6 and quality greater than 1; Reyssat's triple 2 + 3^10 * 109 = 23^5 with rad = 15042 and quality greater than 1.62; 1 + 2 * 3^7 = 5^4 * 7 with rad = 210 and quality greater than 1.56.
  • The exponent 1 + ε cannot be replaced by 1: the triples 1 + (3^(2^k) - 1) = 3^(2^k) satisfy 2^k * rad(abc) < 3 c, because 2^(k + 1) divides 3^(2^k) - 1 (two_pow_mul_rad_family_lt). Hence for every real K there is a coprime triple with c > K * rad(abc) (exists_coprime_triple_gt_mul_rad), and ABCWithExponent 1 is false (not_abcWithExponent_one). This guards against a statement of the conjecture with ε = 0.

The polynomial analog. Mathlib proves the Mason–Stothers theorem (Polynomial.abc, in Mathlib/NumberTheory/FLT/MasonStothers.lean): for nonzero polynomials a, b, c over a field with a and b coprime and a + b + c = 0, either all three derivatives vanish or each degree is less than the degree of radical (a * b * c). masonStothers_natDegree_lt restates it for a + b = c. This is the function field analog of the abc conjecture; it is a theorem, and it implies nothing about the statement for integers.

Public declarations

IUT.ABC.Variants.ABCIntdef

def ABCInt : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ K : ℝ, 0 < K ∧ ∀ a b c : ℤ, a ≠ 0 → b ≠ 0 → c ≠ 0 →
    IsCoprime a b → IsCoprime a c → IsCoprime b c → a + b + c = 0 →
      ((max a.natAbs (max b.natAbs c.natAbs) : ℕ) : ℝ) ≤
        K * (rad (a * b * c).natAbs : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε)

The abc conjecture, signed integer form. For every real ε > 0 there is a real K > 0 such that all nonzero, pairwise coprime integers a, b, c with a + b + c = 0 satisfy max(|a|, |b|, |c|) ≤ K * rad(|abc|)^(1 + ε). The absolute values are Int.natAbs, and the constant and the non-strict inequality are those of Challenge.ABC. Equivalent to Challenge.ABC (abcInt_iff_ABC).

IUT.ABC.Variants.ABCIntSumdef

def ABCIntSum : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ K : ℝ, 0 < K ∧ ∀ a b c : ℤ, a ≠ 0 → b ≠ 0 → c ≠ 0 →
    IsCoprime a b → IsCoprime a c → IsCoprime b c → a + b = c →
      ((max a.natAbs (max b.natAbs c.natAbs) : ℕ) : ℝ) ≤
        K * (rad (a * b * c).natAbs : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε)

**The abc conjecture, signed form with a + b = c.** As ABCInt, with a + b = c in place of a + b + c = 0 and the signs of a, b, c arbitrary. Equivalent to Challenge.ABC (abcIntSum_iff_ABC).

IUT.ABC.Variants.abc_symmetric_of_ABCtheorem

theorem abc_symmetric_of_ABC (h : ABC) {ε : ℝ} (hε : 0 < ε) :
  ∃ K : ℝ, 0 < K ∧ ∀ A B C : ℕ, 0 < A → 0 < B → 0 < C →
    A.Coprime B → A.Coprime C → B.Coprime C → (A + B = C ∨ A + C = B ∨ B + C = A) →
      ((max A (max B C) : ℕ) : ℝ) ≤ K * (rad (A * B * C) : ℝ) ^ (1 + ε)

The constant form, made symmetric: for pairwise coprime positive A, B, C one of which is the sum of the other two, the largest is bounded by K * rad(ABC)^(1 + ε).

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcInt_of_ABCtheorem

theorem abcInt_of_ABC (h : ABC) : ABCInt

The constant form implies the signed integer form. Of three nonzero integers with sum 0, the absolute value of one is the sum of the other two absolute values.

IUT.ABC.Variants.coprime_sum_of_coprimetheorem

theorem coprime_sum_of_coprime {a b c : ℕ} (hab : a.Coprime b) (h : a + b = c) :
  a.Coprime c ∧ b.Coprime c

For positive a, b with a + b = c, coprimality of a and b gives coprimality of a and c and of b and c.

IUT.ABC.Variants.ABC_of_abcInttheorem

theorem ABC_of_abcInt (h : ABCInt) : ABC

The signed integer form implies the constant form: apply it to (a, b, -c).

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcInt_iff_ABCtheorem

theorem abcInt_iff_ABC : ABCInt ↔ ABC

**The signed integer form is equivalent to Challenge.ABC.**

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcIntSum_iff_abcInttheorem

theorem abcIntSum_iff_abcInt : ABCIntSum ↔ ABCInt

The two signed forms are equivalent: replace c by -c.

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcIntSum_iff_ABCtheorem

theorem abcIntSum_iff_ABC : ABCIntSum ↔ ABC

**The signed form with a + b = c is equivalent to Challenge.ABC.**

IUT.ABC.Variants.ABCWithExponentdef

def ABCWithExponent (κ : ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ K : ℝ, 0 < K ∧ ∀ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a → 0 < b → 0 < c → Nat.Coprime a b → a + b = c →
    (c : ℝ) ≤ K * (rad (a * b * c) : ℝ) ^ κ

The bound of the abc conjecture with one fixed exponent κ: there is a real K > 0 such that every coprime triple of positive integers a + b = c satisfies c ≤ K * rad(abc)^κ. The hypotheses, the constant and the non-strict inequality are those of Challenge.ABC. The abc conjecture is ∀ ε > 0, ABCWithExponent (1 + ε) (abc_iff_forall_abcWithExponent). For one fixed κ > 1 this proposition is implied by the conjecture and is an open, strictly weaker statement; it does not give the conjecture. For κ ≤ 1 it is false (not_abcWithExponent_of_le_one).

IUT.ABC.Variants.abc_iff_forall_abcWithExponenttheorem

theorem abc_iff_forall_abcWithExponent : ABC ↔ ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ABCWithExponent (1 + ε)

The abc conjecture is the fixed exponent bound for every exponent 1 + ε with ε > 0.

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcWithExponent_monotheorem

theorem abcWithExponent_mono {κ κ' : ℝ} (h : κ ≤ κ') :
  ABCWithExponent κ → ABCWithExponent κ'

The fixed exponent bound is monotone in the exponent, because rad(abc) ≥ 1.

IUT.ABC.Variants.abcWithExponent_of_ABCtheorem

theorem abcWithExponent_of_ABC (h : ABC) {κ : ℝ} (hκ : 1 < κ) : ABCWithExponent κ

The abc conjecture implies the fixed exponent bound for every exponent κ > 1.

IUT.ABC.Variants.two_le_rad_of_tripletheorem

theorem two_le_rad_of_triple {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  2 ≤ rad (a * b * c)

For positive a, b with a + b = c, rad(abc) ≥ 2.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_le_mul_multheorem

theorem rad_le_mul_mul {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  rad (a * b * c) ≤ a * b * c

For positive a, b with a + b = c, rad(abc) ≤ abc.

IUT.ABC.Variants.mul_mul_lt_cubetheorem

theorem mul_mul_lt_cube {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  a * b * c < c ^ 3

For positive a, b with a + b = c, abc < c^3.

IUT.ABC.Variants.six_le_rad_of_three_letheorem

theorem six_le_rad_of_three_le {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (hab : a.Coprime b)
  (h : a + b = c) (hc : 3 ≤ c) : 6 ≤ rad (a * b * c)

For a coprime triple of positive integers a + b = c with c ≥ 3, rad(abc) ≥ 6: a prime divisor of c and a prime divisor of whichever of a, b is at least 2 are distinct, so their product, which is at least 2 * 3, divides rad(abc). The bound is attained by 1 + 2 = 3.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_one_one_twotheorem

theorem rad_one_one_two : rad (1 * 1 * 2) = 2

The hypothesis c ≥ 3 of six_le_rad_of_three_le is needed: 1 + 1 = 2 has rad(1 * 1 * 2) = 2.

IUT.ABC.Variants.log_mul_mul_lt_three_mul_logtheorem

theorem log_mul_mul_lt_three_mul_log {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (hb : 0 < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  Real.log ((a * b * c : ℕ) : ℝ) < 3 * Real.log c

For positive a, b with a + b = c: log(abc) < 3 log c, that is, the balance is less than 3 (Laniewski and Müller, The quality plane of abc-triples and the decomposition into gains, Lemma 2.2, page 2). No coprimality and no order of a, b is needed.

IUT.ABC.Variants.sq_lt_mul_mul_of_two_letheorem

theorem sq_lt_mul_mul_of_two_le {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 2 ≤ a) (hab : a < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  c ^ 2 < a * b * c

For 2 ≤ a < b with a + b = c: c^2 < abc, because ab ≥ 2b > a + b = c.

IUT.ABC.Variants.two_mul_log_lt_log_mul_multheorem

theorem two_mul_log_lt_log_mul_mul {a b c : ℕ} (ha : 2 ≤ a) (hab : a < b) (h : a + b = c) :
  2 * Real.log c < Real.log ((a * b * c : ℕ) : ℝ)

For 2 ≤ a < b with a + b = c: 2 log c < log(abc), that is, the balance is greater than 2 (Laniewski and Müller, The quality plane of abc-triples and the decomposition into gains, Lemma 2.2, page 2). Coprimality is not needed. Both other hypotheses are needed: not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_one and not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_b.

IUT.ABC.Variants.not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_onetheorem

theorem not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_one : ¬ ((3 : ℕ) ^ 2 < 1 * 2 * 3)

The hypothesis a ≥ 2 of two_mul_log_lt_log_mul_mul is needed: 1 + 2 = 3 has abc = 6 < 9 = c^2.

IUT.ABC.Variants.not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_btheorem

theorem not_sq_lt_of_a_eq_b : ¬ ((4 : ℕ) ^ 2 < 2 * 2 * 4)

The hypothesis a < b of two_mul_log_lt_log_mul_mul is needed: 2 + 2 = 4 has abc = 16 = c^2.

IUT.ABC.Variants.log_mul_mul_lt_two_mul_log_of_onetheorem

theorem log_mul_mul_lt_two_mul_log_of_one {b c : ℕ} (hb : 0 < b) (h : 1 + b = c) :
  Real.log ((1 * b * c : ℕ) : ℝ) < 2 * Real.log c

For 1 + b = c with b > 0: log(abc) < 2 log c, that is, the balance is less than 2 when a = 1 (Laniewski and Müller, The quality plane of abc-triples and the decomposition into gains, Lemma 2.2, page 2).

IUT.ABC.Variants.two_mul_log_sub_lt_log_mul_mul_of_onetheorem

theorem two_mul_log_sub_lt_log_mul_mul_of_one {b c : ℕ} (hb : 2 ≤ b) (h : 1 + b = c) :
  2 * Real.log c - 1 / ((c : ℝ) - 1) < Real.log ((1 * b * c : ℕ) : ℝ)

For 1 + b = c with b ≥ 2: 2 log c - 1 / (c - 1) < log(abc), that is, λ > 2 - 1 / ((c - 1) log c) when a = 1 (Laniewski and Müller, The quality plane of abc-triples and the decomposition into gains, Lemma 2.2, page 2). The proof is theirs: log(c / (c - 1)) < c / (c - 1) - 1 = 1 / (c - 1).

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_of_primetheorem

theorem rad_of_prime {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) : rad p = p

The radical of a prime is the prime.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_one_eight_ninetheorem

theorem rad_one_eight_nine : rad (1 * 8 * 9) = 6

1 + 8 = 9: the radical of 1 * 8 * 9 is 6.

IUT.ABC.Variants.one_lt_quality_one_eight_ninetheorem

theorem one_lt_quality_one_eight_nine : 1 < FormalConjectures.quality 1 8 9

1 + 8 = 9 has quality log 9 / log 6 > 1.

IUT.ABC.Variants.reyssat_tripletheorem

theorem reyssat_triple : 2 + 3 ^ 10 * 109 = 23 ^ 5 ∧ Nat.Coprime 2 (3 ^ 10 * 109)

Reyssat's triple 2 + 3^10 * 109 = 23^5 is a coprime triple.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_reyssattheorem

theorem rad_reyssat : rad (2 * (3 ^ 10 * 109) * 23 ^ 5) = 15042

Reyssat's triple has radical 2 * 3 * 109 * 23 = 15042.

IUT.ABC.Variants.quality_reyssat_gttheorem

theorem quality_reyssat_gt : (1.62 : ℝ) < FormalConjectures.quality 2 (3 ^ 10 * 109) (23 ^ 5)

Reyssat's triple has quality greater than 1.62: 81 log 15042 < 50 log 23^5, checked as 15042^81 < (23^5)^50.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_one_two_three_pow_seventheorem

theorem rad_one_two_three_pow_seven :
  1 + 2 * 3 ^ 7 = 5 ^ 4 * 7 ∧ rad (1 * (2 * 3 ^ 7) * (5 ^ 4 * 7)) = 210

The triple 1 + 2 * 3^7 = 5^4 * 7 is a coprime triple with radical 2 * 3 * 5 * 7 = 210.

IUT.ABC.Variants.quality_one_two_three_pow_seven_gttheorem

theorem quality_one_two_three_pow_seven_gt :
  (1.56 : ℝ) < FormalConjectures.quality 1 (2 * 3 ^ 7) (5 ^ 4 * 7)

The triple 1 + 2 * 3^7 = 5^4 * 7 has quality greater than 1.56: 39 log 210 < 25 log 4375.

IUT.ABC.Variants.three_pow_two_pow_eq_two_pow_mul_add_onetheorem

theorem three_pow_two_pow_eq_two_pow_mul_add_one (k : ℕ) :
  ∃ m : ℕ, 0 < m ∧ 3 ^ 2 ^ k = 2 ^ (k + 1) * m + 1

2^(k + 1) divides 3^(2^k) - 1, in the form 3^(2^k) = 2^(k + 1) * m + 1 with m > 0. The step is x^2 - 1 = (x - 1)(x + 1) with x + 1 even.

IUT.ABC.Variants.rad_mul_le_mul_radtheorem

theorem rad_mul_le_mul_rad (x y : ℕ) : rad (x * y) ≤ rad x * rad y

The radical of a product is at most the product of the radicals.

IUT.ABC.Variants.two_pow_mul_rad_family_lttheorem

theorem two_pow_mul_rad_family_lt (k : ℕ) :
  2 ^ k * rad (1 * (3 ^ 2 ^ k - 1) * 3 ^ 2 ^ k) < 3 * 3 ^ 2 ^ k

The triples 1 + (3^(2^k) - 1) = 3^(2^k) satisfy 2^k * rad(abc) < 3 c. With 3^(2^k) - 1 = 2^(k + 1) m, the radical is at most 2 * m * 3.

IUT.ABC.Variants.exists_coprime_triple_gt_mul_radtheorem

theorem exists_coprime_triple_gt_mul_rad (K : ℝ) :
  ∃ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ a.Coprime b ∧ a + b = c ∧
    K * (rad (a * b * c) : ℝ) < c

**For every real K there is a coprime triple of positive integers a + b = c with c > K * rad(abc).** Take k with 2^k > 3 K in two_pow_mul_rad_family_lt.

IUT.ABC.Variants.not_abcWithExponent_onetheorem

theorem not_abcWithExponent_one : ¬ ABCWithExponent 1

**The bound with exponent 1 is false**: no constant K gives c ≤ K * rad(abc) for all coprime triples. So ε > 0 in the abc conjecture cannot be dropped.

IUT.ABC.Variants.not_abcWithExponent_of_le_onetheorem

theorem not_abcWithExponent_of_le_one {κ : ℝ} (h : κ ≤ 1) : ¬ ABCWithExponent κ

The bound with any exponent κ ≤ 1 is false.

IUT.ABC.Variants.masonStothers_natDegree_lttheorem

theorem masonStothers_natDegree_lt {k : Type*} [Field k] [DecidableEq k] {a b c : Polynomial k}
  (ha : a ≠ 0) (hb : b ≠ 0) (hc : c ≠ 0) (hab : IsCoprime a b) (hsum : a + b = c)
  (hder : ¬ (Polynomial.derivative a = 0 ∧ Polynomial.derivative b = 0 ∧
    Polynomial.derivative c = 0)) :
  c.natDegree < (UniqueFactorizationMonoid.radical (a * b * c)).natDegree

**Mason–Stothers for a + b = c.** For nonzero polynomials over a field with a and b coprime, a + b = c, and not all three derivatives zero, the degree of c is less than the degree of the radical of abc. This restates Mathlib's Polynomial.abc (stated there for a + b + c = 0). It is the function field analog of the abc conjecture, a theorem, and it implies nothing about the integer statement.

Prerequisites

General mathematics needed by the papers.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog

IUT/Prerequisites/PadicLog.lean

The p-adic logarithm on ℚ_p and the log-shell of ℚ_p

Module documentation

Sources:

  • Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory III, Definition 1.1, PDF pages 23 and 24 (rendered and read): for a nonarchimedean v, the pre-log-shell is the image under the p_v-adic logarithm of the Galois-invariant units, and the log-shell is the pre-log-shell multiplied by (p_v^*)⁻¹, with p_v^* = p_v for odd p_v and p_v^* = p_v ^ 2 for p_v = 2 (page 24, citing [AbsTopIII], Definition 5.4 (iii)).
  • Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory III, Proposition 1.2 (v), PDF pages 31 and 32 (rendered and read): at nonarchimedean v the log-shell is compact, contains the invariants of the integers of the codomain, and contains the image of the invariant units.
  • Mochizuki, Inter-universal Teichmüller Theory IV, Proposition 1.2 (i), PDF pages 10 and 11 (rendered and read; page 9, read, fixes the notation R_i, ord, p ^ λ of Proposition 1.1 used there): for a finite extension k_i of ℚ_p with ramification index e_i, p ^ (a_i) R_i ⊆ log_p (R_i^×) ⊆ p ^ (- b_i) R_i, with equalities when p > 2 and e_i ≤ p - 2; the proof cites [Kobl], page 81.

Pages read for this file: IUT III pages 23, 24, 31 and 32; IUT IV pages 9, 10 and 11. Pages checked against rendered images: the same.

This file is a prerequisite for log-shells over ℚ_p only. Finite extensions of ℚ_p, tensor products of rings of integers, and the Frobenioid-theoretic setting of IUT III are not treated. It does not formalize IUT III Definition 1.1, IUT III Proposition 1.2 or IUT IV Proposition 1.2; it proves, for the base field k = ℚ_p (so e = 1, R = ℤ_p), the facts about the logarithm on which they rest.

Contents
  • padicLogOnePlus p x = ∑_{n ≥ 1} (-1) ^ (n + 1) x ^ n / n, which agrees with Mathlib's NormedSpace.log (1 + x) (padicLogOnePlus_eq_normedSpace_log) and converges for ‖x‖ < 1 (padicLogSeries_summable). Outside the open unit disc the value is a junk value.
  • ‖log (1 + x)‖ = ‖x‖ for ‖x‖ ≤ 1 / p* (padicLogOnePlus_norm_eq), that is ‖x‖ ≤ 1 / p for odd p and ‖x‖ ≤ 1 / 4 for p = 2.
  • The homomorphism property log ((1 + x) (1 + y)) = log (1 + x) + log (1 + y) on the whole open unit disc (padicLogOnePlus_add_add_mul), proved by re-expanding the series around x as a norm-summable double series and summing it in both orders.
  • The logarithm of a unit, padicLogUnit p u = (p - 1)⁻¹ log (u ^ (p - 1)), which agrees with log u on 1 + p ℤ_p (padicLogUnit_one_add), and its image log (ℤ_p^×) = {z | ‖z‖ ≤ 1 / p*} (padicLogUnit_range), that is p ℤ_p for odd p and 4 ℤ_2 for p = 2. Surjectivity is proved by the contraction mapping principle.
  • The log-shell padicLogShell p = (p*)⁻¹ · log (ℤ_p^×) and its computation padicLogShell p = ℤ_p (padicLogShell_eq_range_coe), with the three properties of IUT III Proposition 1.2 (v) at nonarchimedean v in this case: compactness (padicLogShell_isCompact), ℤ_p ⊆ I (padicInt_subset_padicLogShell) and log (ℤ_p^×) ⊆ I (padicLogUnit_range_subset_padicLogShell).
Interpretation choices, recorded
  • The "Galois-invariant units" of IUT III page 24 are taken to be ℤ_p^× for the base field ℚ_p (the invariants of the units of the ring of integers of an algebraic closure under the absolute Galois group of ℚ_p); this identification is not formalized here.
  • The papers write log_p without fixing the extension to all units on the pages read. The extension (p - 1)⁻¹ log (u ^ (p - 1)) used here is the standard one; for p = 2 it is log u itself. Since log is a homomorphism on 1 + p ℤ_p (padicLogOnePlus_add_add_mul), this extension is a homomorphism on ℤ_p^× (padicLogUnit_mul), and it is the unique homomorphic extension into the torsion-free group ℚ_p; that uniqueness statement is not formalized here.
  • For k = ℚ_p, the constants of IUT IV Proposition 1.2 are a = 1, b = -1 for p > 2 and a = 2, b = 0 for p = 2 (computed from the displays on page 10). The theorem padicLogUnit_range gives the equality log (ℤ_p^×) = p ℤ_p asserted there for p > 2 and, for p = 2, the sharper log (ℤ_2^×) = 4 ℤ_2, which implies the two inclusions 4 ℤ_2 ⊆ log (ℤ_2^×) ⊆ ℤ_2.
  • Some secondary sources write the log-shell as (2p)⁻¹ log (O_k^×). For p = 2 this is (p*)⁻¹; for odd p, 2p and p* = p differ by the unit 2, so the set is the same. Only the (p*)⁻¹ form of page 24 is defined; padicLogShell_eq_two_mul proves the two forms equal.
Public declarations
variable {p : ℕ} [hp : Fact p.Prime]

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogSeriesdef

noncomputable def padicLogSeries (p : ℕ) [Fact p.Prime] (x : ℚ_[p]) (n : ℕ) : ℚ_[p] :=
  (-1) ^ (n + 1) / n * x ^ n

The n-th term (-1) ^ (n + 1) x ^ n / n of the logarithm series; the 0-th term is 0 because 1 / 0 = 0.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlusdef

noncomputable def padicLogOnePlus (p : ℕ) [Fact p.Prime] (x : ℚ_[p]) : ℚ_[p] :=
  ∑' n : ℕ, padicLogSeries p x n

log (1 + x) = ∑_{n ≥ 1} (-1) ^ (n + 1) x ^ n / n; meaningful for ‖x‖ < 1, where the series converges (padicLogSeries_summable), and a junk value otherwise.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_eq_normedSpace_logtheorem

theorem padicLogOnePlus_eq_normedSpace_log (x : ℚ_[p]) :
  padicLogOnePlus p x = NormedSpace.log (1 + x)

Agreement with Mathlib's NormedSpace.log.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_exists_norm_inv_natCasttheorem

theorem padicLog_exists_norm_inv_natCast {n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) :
  ∃ e : ℕ, p ^ e ∣ n ∧ ‖((n : ℚ_[p]))⁻¹‖ = (p : ℝ) ^ e

For n ≠ 0, ‖1 / n‖ = p ^ e for some e with p ^ e ∣ n.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_norm_inv_natCast_letheorem

theorem padicLog_norm_inv_natCast_le (n : ℕ) : ‖((n : ℚ_[p]))⁻¹‖ ≤ n

‖1 / n‖ ≤ n in ℚ_p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogSeries_norm_letheorem

theorem padicLogSeries_norm_le (x : ℚ_[p]) (n : ℕ) :
  ‖padicLogSeries p x n‖ ≤ n * ‖x‖ ^ n

‖x ^ n / n‖ ≤ n ‖x‖ ^ n.

The logarithm series converges on the open unit disc.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_zerotheorem

log 1 = 0.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogPStardef

def padicLogPStar (p : ℕ) : ℕ := if p = 2 then p ^ 2 else p

p* of IUT III page 24: p for odd p and p ^ 2 = 4 for p = 2.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_aux_lt_three_powtheorem

theorem padicLog_aux_lt_three_pow (m : ℕ) : m + 2 < 3 ^ (m + 1)

An elementary inequality: m + 2 < 3 ^ (m + 1).

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_pow_mul_le_pStar_powtheorem

theorem padicLog_pow_mul_le_pStar_pow {n e : ℕ} (hn : 2 ≤ n) (hdvd : p ^ e ∣ n) :
  p ^ e * p ≤ padicLogPStar p ^ (n - 1)

Key arithmetic: for n ≥ 2 and p ^ e ∣ n, p ^ e * p ≤ (p*) ^ (n - 1).

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogSeries_norm_le_of_two_letheorem

theorem padicLogSeries_norm_le_of_two_le {x : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ ≤ (padicLogPStar p : ℝ)⁻¹)
  {n : ℕ} (hn : 2 ≤ n) : ‖padicLogSeries p x n‖ ≤ ‖x‖ / p

For n ≥ 2 and ‖x‖ ≤ 1 / p*, the n-th term has norm at most ‖x‖ / p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_eq_add_tailtheorem

theorem padicLogOnePlus_eq_add_tail {x : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ < 1) :
  padicLogOnePlus p x = x + ∑' n : ℕ, padicLogSeries p x (n + 2)

log (1 + x) = x + ∑_{n ≥ 2} (-1) ^ (n + 1) x ^ n / n for ‖x‖ < 1.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_norm_eqtheorem

  ‖padicLogOnePlus p x‖ = ‖x‖

‖log (1 + x)‖ = ‖x‖ for ‖x‖ ≤ 1 / p*.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogDoubledef

noncomputable def padicLogDouble (p : ℕ) [Fact p.Prime] (x w : ℚ_[p]) (nk : ℕ × ℕ) : ℚ_[p] :=
  (-1) ^ (nk.1 + 1) / nk.1 * (nk.1.choose nk.2) * x ^ (nk.1 - nk.2) * w ^ nk.2

The double family used to re-expand log (1 + x + w) around x.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogDouble_summabletheorem

theorem padicLogDouble_summable {x w : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ < 1) (hw : ‖w‖ < 1) :
  Summable (padicLogDouble p x w)

The double family is norm-summable for ‖x‖, ‖w‖ < 1.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogDouble_hasSum_fsttheorem

theorem padicLogDouble_hasSum_fst (x w : ℚ_[p]) (n : ℕ) :
  HasSum (fun k => padicLogDouble p x w (n, k)) (padicLogSeries p (x + w) n)

Summing the double family over k first gives the series of log (1 + (x + w)).

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogDouble_hasSum_snd_succtheorem

theorem padicLogDouble_hasSum_snd_succ {x : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ < 1) (w : ℚ_[p]) (j : ℕ) :
  HasSum (fun n => padicLogDouble p x w (n, j + 1))
    ((-1) ^ (j + 2) / (j + 1 : ℕ) * (w / (1 + x)) ^ (j + 1))

Summing the double family over n first, for fixed k ≥ 1.

‖1 + x‖ = 1 for ‖x‖ < 1.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_add_add_multheorem

  padicLogOnePlus p (x + y + x * y) = padicLogOnePlus p x + padicLogOnePlus p y

Homomorphism property on the open unit disc: log ((1 + x) (1 + y)) = log (1 + x) + log (1 + y) for ‖x‖, ‖y‖ < 1.

The ultrametric inequality for a difference.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_sub_sub_norm_letheorem

theorem padicLogOnePlus_sub_sub_norm_le {a b : ℚ_[p]} (ha : ‖a‖ ≤ (padicLogPStar p : ℝ)⁻¹)
  (hb : ‖b‖ ≤ (padicLogPStar p : ℝ)⁻¹) :
  ‖(a - b) - (padicLogOnePlus p a - padicLogOnePlus p b)‖ ≤ ‖a - b‖ / p

Key contraction estimate: for a, b in the closed ball of radius 1 / p*, ‖(a - b) - (log (1 + a) - log (1 + b))‖ ≤ ‖a - b‖ / p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_surjOntheorem

theorem padicLogOnePlus_surjOn {z : ℚ_[p]} (hz : ‖z‖ ≤ (padicLogPStar p : ℝ)⁻¹) :
  ∃ x : ℚ_[p], ‖x‖ ≤ (padicLogPStar p : ℝ)⁻¹ ∧ padicLogOnePlus p x = z

Surjectivity of x ↦ log (1 + x) onto the closed ball of radius 1 / p*.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogOnePlus_pow_sub_onetheorem

theorem padicLogOnePlus_pow_sub_one {x : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ < 1) (n : ℕ) :
  ‖(1 + x) ^ n - 1‖ < 1 ∧ padicLogOnePlus p ((1 + x) ^ n - 1) = n * padicLogOnePlus p x

log ((1 + x) ^ n) = n log (1 + x) on the open unit disc.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogUnitdef

noncomputable def padicLogUnit (p : ℕ) [Fact p.Prime] (u : ℚ_[p]) : ℚ_[p] :=
  ((p - 1 : ℕ) : ℚ_[p])⁻¹ * padicLogOnePlus p (u ^ (p - 1) - 1)

The p-adic logarithm of a unit u of ℤ_p, as (p - 1)⁻¹ log (u ^ (p - 1)), where log is the series padicLogOnePlus at u ^ (p - 1) - 1. Meaningful for ‖u‖ = 1.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogUnit_one_addtheorem

theorem padicLogUnit_one_add {x : ℚ_[p]} (hx : ‖x‖ < 1) :
  padicLogUnit p (1 + x) = padicLogOnePlus p x

On 1 + x with ‖x‖ < 1, the logarithm of units agrees with the series log (1 + x).

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_norm_le_inv_of_toZMod_eq_zerotheorem

  ‖(z : ℚ_[p])‖ ≤ (p : ℝ)⁻¹

An element of ℤ_p with zero residue has norm at most 1 / p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogUnit_rangetheorem

theorem padicLogUnit_range :
  Set.range (fun u : ℤ_[p]ˣ => padicLogUnit p ((u : ℤ_[p]) : ℚ_[p])) =
    {z : ℚ_[p] | ‖z‖ ≤ ((padicLogPStar p : ℕ) : ℝ)⁻¹}

Image of the logarithm on units: log (ℤ_p^×) is the closed ball of radius 1 / p*, that is p ℤ_p for odd p and 4 ℤ_2 for p = 2.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogUnit_range_of_ne_twotheorem

theorem padicLogUnit_range_of_ne_two (h2 : p ≠ 2) :

    {z : ℚ_[p] | ‖z‖ ≤ (p : ℝ)⁻¹}

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogUnit_range_two_boundstheorem

theorem padicLogUnit_range_two_bounds :
  {z : ℚ_[2] | ‖z‖ ≤ (4 : ℝ)⁻¹} ⊆
      Set.range (fun u : ℤ_[2]ˣ => padicLogUnit 2 ((u : ℤ_[2]) : ℚ_[2])) ∧

      {z : ℚ_[2] | ‖z‖ ≤ 1}

IUT IV Proposition 1.2 (i) for k_i = ℚ_2 (a = 2, b = 0): 4 ℤ_2 ⊆ log (ℤ_2^×) ⊆ ℤ_2, written with closed balls.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogShelldef

noncomputable def padicLogShell (p : ℕ) [Fact p.Prime] : Set ℚ_[p] :=
  (fun z => ((padicLogPStar p : ℕ) : ℚ_[p])⁻¹ * z) ''
    Set.range (fun u : ℤ_[p]ˣ => padicLogUnit p ((u : ℤ_[p]) : ℚ_[p]))

The log-shell of ℚ_p in the sense of IUT III Definition 1.1, page 24, for the base field ℚ_p: (p*)⁻¹ · log (ℤ_p^×).

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLog_image_inv_mul_rangetheorem

theorem padicLog_image_inv_mul_range {c : ℚ_[p]} (hc : ‖c‖ = ((padicLogPStar p : ℕ) : ℝ)⁻¹) :
  (fun z => c⁻¹ * z) '' Set.range (fun u : ℤ_[p]ˣ => padicLogUnit p ((u : ℤ_[p]) : ℚ_[p])) =
    {y : ℚ_[p] | ‖y‖ ≤ 1}

The log-shell of ℚ_p is the closed unit ball, that is ℤ_p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogShell_eqtheorem

theorem padicLogShell_eq : padicLogShell p = {y : ℚ_[p] | ‖y‖ ≤ 1}

The log-shell of ℚ_p is the closed unit ball of ℚ_p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogShell_eq_two_multheorem

theorem padicLogShell_eq_two_mul :
  (fun z => ((2 * p : ℕ) : ℚ_[p])⁻¹ * z) ''
      Set.range (fun u : ℤ_[p]ˣ => padicLogUnit p ((u : ℤ_[p]) : ℚ_[p])) =
    padicLogShell p

The variant with the factor (2p)⁻¹ in place of (p*)⁻¹ gives the same set: for odd p, 2p and p* = p have the same norm, and for p = 2, 2p = 4 = p*.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicLogShell_eq_range_coetheorem

theorem padicLogShell_eq_range_coe :
  padicLogShell p = Set.range ((↑) : ℤ_[p] → ℚ_[p])

The log-shell of ℚ_p equals ℤ_p, viewed inside ℚ_p.

IUT.Prerequisites.PadicLog.padicInt_subset_padicLogShelltheorem

theorem padicInt_subset_padicLogShell : Set.range ((↑) : ℤ_[p] → ℚ_[p]) ⊆ padicLogShell p

IUT III Proposition 1.2 (v) (b_non), page 32, for the base field ℚ_p: the log-shell contains the integers ℤ_p (the invariants of the integers of the codomain), viewed inside ℚ_p.
